<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Alexandra_Feodorovna_with_Rasputin,_her_children_and_a_governess.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d7/Alexandra_Feodorovna_with_Rasputin%2C_her_children_and_a_governess.jpg/250px-Alexandra_Feodorovna_with_Rasputin%2C_her_children_and_a_governess.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="248" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d7/Alexandra_Feodorovna_with_Rasputin%2C_her_children_and_a_governess.jpg/375px-Alexandra_Feodorovna_with_Rasputin%2C_her_children_and_a_governess.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d7/Alexandra_Feodorovna_with_Rasputin%2C_her_children_and_a_governess.jpg/500px-Alexandra_Feodorovna_with_Rasputin%2C_her_children_and_a_governess.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1936" data-file-height="1920" /></a><figcaption>Alexandra Feodorovna with Rasputin, her children and a governess, 1908</figcaption></figure>
<p>Tsarevich Alexei Nikolaevich of Russia was heir apparent to the throne of Russia and the only son of Nicholas and Alexandra. Shortly after his birth, the court doctors realized that he had <a href="/wiki/Haemophilia" title="Haemophilia">haemophilia</a>. After his umbilical cord was cut, his stomach bled for days and his blood did not clot. Nicholas wrote that Alexei lost "1/8 to 1/9 of the total quantity" of his blood in 48 hours.<sup id="cite_ref-The_Romanovs,_p._519_101-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-The_Romanovs,_p._519-101">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> Haemophilia had entered the royal houses of Europe via the daughters of Queen Victoria, including Alexandra's mother, Princess Alice.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> In the early 20th century, haemophilia was fatal and the average life expectancy of haemophiliacs was age 13. Alexandra's brother, <a href="/wiki/Prince_Friedrich_of_Hesse_and_by_Rhine" title="Prince Friedrich of Hesse and by Rhine">Friedrich</a>, and maternal uncle <a href="/wiki/Prince_Leopold,_Duke_of_Albany" title="Prince Leopold, Duke of Albany">Prince Leopold, Duke of Albany</a>, had died young of haemophilia. Alexandra's sister <a href="/wiki/Princess_Irene_of_Hesse_and_by_Rhine" title="Princess Irene of Hesse and by Rhine">Princess Irene of Hesse and by Rhine</a> and first cousin <a href="/wiki/Victoria_Eugenie_of_Battenberg" title="Victoria Eugenie of Battenberg">Princess Victoria Eugenie of Battenberg</a> were also carriers of the haemophilia gene, and they had haemophiliac sons.
</p><p>Alexandra felt immense guilt that she had passed down the disease to her son. Shortly after Alexei's diagnosis, she wept and told the nurse, "If only you knew how fervently I've prayed for God to protect my son from our inherited curse."<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> Nicholas' sister Xenia called haemophilia "the terrible disease of the English family",<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> and members of the Imperial family blamed Alexandra for "contaminating the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Romanov" title="House of Romanov">Romanovs</a> with the diseases of her own race."<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Since the incurable illness threatened the sole son and heir of the emperor, the imperial family decided to keep his condition secret from the Russian people. They wanted to limit social instability because of uncertainty. At first, Alexandra turned to Russian doctors to treat Alexei. Their treatments generally failed. Burdened with the threats to her son from any fall or cut, Alexandra turned toward faith for comfort. She studied the Orthodox faith and saints, and spent hours daily praying in her private chapel for deliverance.<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Grigori_Rasputin" title="Grigori Rasputin">Grigori Rasputin</a>, a peasant from Siberia, appeared to have a cure for her son by praying for him and became powerful in court as a result. Over time, Alexandra grew to believe that Rasputin was the only man who could save her son's life. Rasputin was straightforward with Alexandra and told her, "Neither the Emperor nor you can do without me. If I am not there to protect you, you will lose your son... within six months."<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> Alexandra blinded herself to evidence of Rasputin's debauchery and the harm his presence did to Imperial prestige. The director of the national police told Alexandra that a drunk Rasputin had exposed himself at a popular Moscow restaurant and bragged that Nicholas gave him sexual access to her, but she blamed the account on malicious gossip. "Saints are always calumniated," she once wrote. "He is hated because we love him."<sup id="cite_ref-Denton,_C.S_p.577_108-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Denton,_C.S_p.577-108">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> Nicholas recognized Rasputin's faults, but he felt powerless to do anything about the man who seemingly saved his only son's life. <a href="/wiki/Pierre_Gilliard" title="Pierre Gilliard">Pierre Gilliard</a> wrote, "He did not like to send Rasputin away, for if Alexei died, in the eyes of the mother, he would have been the murderer of his own son."<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability#Self-published_sources" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability"><span title="The material near this tag may rely on a self-published source. (November 2021)">self-published source</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>From the start, members of the court exchanged gossip about Rasputin. Although some of St. Petersburg's top clergy accepted him as a living prophet, others angrily denounced him as a fraud and a heretic. Made up stories from his life in Siberia were heard in St. Petersburg. For instance, he was said to conduct weddings for villagers in exchange for sleeping on the first night with the bride. He lived in St. Petersburg with his two daughters and two housekeepers, and was often visited by persons seeking his blessing, a healing, or a favour with the tsarina. Women, enchanted by the healer, also came to Rasputin for advice and individual blessings and received a private audience in his apartment, jokingly called the "Holy of Holies". Rasputin liked to preach a unique theology that one must become familiar with sin before having a chance to overcome it.<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> No one believed that Rasputin could heal Alexei, so court officials were confused as to why Alexandra was so dependent on him.
</p><p>In 1912, Alexei suffered a life-threatening <a href="/wiki/Bleeding" title="Bleeding">haemorrhage</a> in the thigh while the family was at <a href="/wiki/Spa%C5%82a" title="Spała">Spała</a> in Poland. Alexandra sat for days at his bedside, and she rarely ate or slept.<sup id="cite_ref-Denton,_C.S_p.575_111-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Denton,_C.S_p.575-111">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> She cried helplessly when Alexei begged for death and asked her to bury him in a forest instead of the mausoleum with his Romanov ancestors. The doctors expected Alexei to die, and a priest performed his last rites. The court officials prepared an official telegram to announce the death of the Tsarevich. In desperation, Alexandra sent a telegram to Rasputin, who replied: "God has seen your tears and heard your prayers. Do not grieve. The Little One will not die. Do not allow the doctors to bother him too much."<sup id="cite_ref-Denton,_C.S_p.575_111-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Denton,_C.S_p.575-111">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> To the shock of his doctors, Alexei recovered his health and survived. From 1912, Alexandra came to rely increasingly on Rasputin and to believe in his ability to ease Alexei's suffering. It looked like this reliance enhanced Rasputin's political power, but it is hard to detach gossips from the truth. His role in the court seriously undermined Romanov rule during the First World War.
</p><p>Rasputin was assassinated to end his perceived interference in political matters, on 30 December 1916. Amongst the conspirators were the nobleman <a href="/wiki/Felix_Yusupov" title="Felix Yusupov">Prince Felix Yusupov</a>, who was married to Nicholas II's niece, <a href="/wiki/Princess_Irina_of_Russia" class="mw-redirect" title="Princess Irina of Russia">Princess Irina of Russia</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duke_Dmitri_Pavlovich_of_Russia" title="Grand Duke Dmitri Pavlovich of Russia">Grand Duke Dmitri Pavlovich</a>, who was once close to Nicholas and Alexandra's family.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="World_War_I">World War I</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alexandra_Feodorovna_(Alix_of_Hesse)&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: World War I">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Alix_h%C3%A1bor%C3%BA.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7e/Alix_h%C3%A1bor%C3%BA.jpg/220px-Alix_h%C3%A1bor%C3%BA.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="311" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7e/Alix_h%C3%A1bor%C3%BA.jpg/330px-Alix_h%C3%A1bor%C3%BA.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7e/Alix_h%C3%A1bor%C3%BA.jpg/440px-Alix_h%C3%A1bor%C3%BA.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3625" data-file-height="5126" /></a><figcaption>Alexandra Fyodorovna in her Red Cross nursing uniform during the war, 1916.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The outbreak of <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a> was a pivotal moment for Russia and Alexandra. The war pitted the Russian Empire of the Romanov dynasty against the German Empire of the <a href="/wiki/Hohenzollern_dynasty" class="mw-redirect" title="Hohenzollern dynasty">Hohenzollern dynasty</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> When Alexandra learned of the Russian mobilization, she stormed into her husband's study and said: "War! And I knew nothing of it! This is the end of everything."<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Alexandra's ties to Germany made her more unpopular among some societies in Russia. Her brother <a href="/wiki/Ernest_Louis,_Grand_Duke_of_Hesse" title="Ernest Louis, Grand Duke of Hesse">Ernest Louis</a> ruled the <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duchy_of_Hesse_and_by_Rhine" class="mw-redirect" title="Grand Duchy of Hesse and by Rhine">Grand Duchy of Hesse and by Rhine</a>, so he fought with the Germans. The German Emperor, <a href="/wiki/Wilhelm_II,_German_Emperor" title="Wilhelm II, German Emperor">Wilhelm II</a>, was Alexandra's first cousin. Alexandra's sister, <a href="/wiki/Princess_Irene_of_Hesse_and_by_Rhine" title="Princess Irene of Hesse and by Rhine">Irene</a>, was married to Wilhelm's brother, <a href="/wiki/Prince_Henry_of_Prussia_(1862%E2%80%931929)" title="Prince Henry of Prussia (1862–1929)">Heinrich</a>. Ironically, Alexandra was an ardent Russian patriot and disliked the German Emperor. She privately wrote that Wilhelm II "is really nothing but a clown. He has no real worth. His only virtues are his strict morals and his conjugal fidelity."<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>St. Petersburg's society accused her of collaboration with the Germans.<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> In St. Petersburg, there was a rumor that Alexandra was hiding her brother Ernie in Russia. In 1916, Alexandra's lady-in-waiting wrote that she was asked "in all seriousness whether the Grand Duke of Hesse was not hidden in the cellars of the palace."<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> Alexandra worked as a nurse to wounded soldiers, but her efforts went unappreciated. In St. Petersburg, there were rumors that Alexandra and Rasputin were carrying on nightly conversations with Wilhelm II in Berlin to negotiate a dishonorable peace.<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>When Nicholas travelled to the front line in 1915 to take personal command of the Army, he left Alexandra in charge as Regent in the capital Saint Petersburg. Her brother-in-law, <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duke_Alexander_Mikhailovich_of_Russia" title="Grand Duke Alexander Mikhailovich of Russia">Grand Duke Alexander Mikhailovich</a> recorded, "When the Emperor went to war of course his wife governed instead of him."<sup id="cite_ref-King,_G_p.244_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-King,_G_p.244-118">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>It looked like Alexandra fired and appointed ministers based on Rasputin's self-serving advice, but those close to the Imperial family circle have denied this. In only sixteen months, she appointed four prime ministers, five ministers of interior, and three ministers of war.<sup id="cite_ref-Robert_Massie_p._348_119-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Robert_Massie_p._348-119">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup>  "After the middle of 1915," wrote Florinsky, "the fairly honorable and efficient group who formed the top of the bureaucratic pyramid degenerated into a rapidly changing succession of the appointees of Rasputin."<sup id="cite_ref-Robert_Massie_p._348_119-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Robert_Massie_p._348-119">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Alexei_Polivanov" title="Alexei Polivanov">Polivanov</a> was an excellent official who was credited with revitalizing the Russian army, but Alexandra declared, "I don't like the choice of Minister of War Polivanov. Is he not our Friend's [Rasputin's] enemy?"<sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup>  The general <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duke_Nicholas_Nikolaevich_of_Russia_(1856%E2%80%931929)" title="Grand Duke Nicholas Nikolaevich of Russia (1856–1929)">Grand Duke Nicholas Nikolaevich</a> disliked Rasputin, because Rasputin saw and told Alexandra that the Grand Duke was deliberately currying favor in the army and overshadowing Nicholas II so that he could claim the throne. On 16 June, Alexandra wrote to Nicholas, "I have absolutely no faith in N.... [he has] gone against a Man of God (Rasputin), his work can't be blessed or his advice good... Russia will not be blessed if her sovereign lets a Man of God sent to help him be persecuted, I am sure."<sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> She insisted to Nicholas that "[Rasputin] has your interest and Russia's at heart. It is not for nothing God sent him to us, only we must pay more attention to what He says. His words are not lightly spoken and the importance of having not only his prayers but his advice is great."<sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ever a believer in autocracy, Alexandra persuaded Nicholas that he must never relinquish his absolute power as Emperor. She wrote to him: "You are master and sovereign of Russia. Almighty God set you in place, and they should all bow down before your wisdom and steadfastness."<sup id="cite_ref-Carolly_Erickson_p.247_123-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Carolly_Erickson_p.247-123">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> She advised him to "Be Peter the Great, Ivan the Terrible, Emperor Paul-- crush them all."<sup id="cite_ref-Carolly_Erickson_p.247_123-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Carolly_Erickson_p.247-123">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> She criticized the Duma and declared "they want to discuss things not concerning them and bring more discontent—they must be kept away.... We are not ready for constitutional government."<sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the war, there was great concern within the imperial house about the influence empress Alexandra had upon state affairs through the Tsar, and the influence <a href="/wiki/Grigori_Rasputin" title="Grigori Rasputin">Grigori Rasputin</a> was believed to have upon her, as it was considered to provoke the public and endanger the safety of the imperial throne and the survival of the monarchy.<sup id="cite_ref-King,_Greg_1994._p._299-300_125-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-King,_Greg_1994._p._299-300-125">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> On behalf of the Imperial relatives of the Tsar, both <a href="/wiki/Princess_Elisabeth_of_Hesse_and_by_Rhine_(1864%E2%80%931918)" title="Princess Elisabeth of Hesse and by Rhine (1864–1918)">Grand Duchess Elizabeth Feodorovna</a> and <a href="/wiki/Princess_Victoria_Melita_of_Saxe-Coburg_and_Gotha" title="Princess Victoria Melita of Saxe-Coburg and Gotha">Grand Duchess Victoria Feodorovna</a> had been selected to mediate and ask Empress Alexandra to banish Rasputin from court to protect her and the throne's reputation, the former twice, but without success. In parallel, several of the Grand Dukes had tried to intervene with the Tsar, but with no more success.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Nicholas_II_with_his_family_in_Yevpatoria.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c0/Nicholas_II_with_his_family_in_Yevpatoria.jpg/220px-Nicholas_II_with_his_family_in_Yevpatoria.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="157" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c0/Nicholas_II_with_his_family_in_Yevpatoria.jpg/330px-Nicholas_II_with_his_family_in_Yevpatoria.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c0/Nicholas_II_with_his_family_in_Yevpatoria.jpg/440px-Nicholas_II_with_his_family_in_Yevpatoria.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5183" data-file-height="3707" /></a><figcaption>The Tsar, Alexandra and their children in <a href="/wiki/Yevpatoria" title="Yevpatoria">Yevpatoria</a>, Crimea, May 1916</figcaption></figure>
<p>During this conflict of 1916–1917, <a href="/wiki/Duchess_Marie_of_Mecklenburg-Schwerin" title="Duchess Marie of Mecklenburg-Schwerin">Grand Duchess Maria Pavlovna</a> reportedly planned a <a href="/wiki/Coup_d%27%C3%A9tat" title="Coup d&#39;état">coup d'état</a> to depose the Tsar with the help of four regiments of the Imperial guard which were to invade the Alexander Palace, force the Tsar to abdicate and replace him with his underage son under the regency of her son <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duke_Kirill_Vladimirovich_of_Russia" title="Grand Duke Kirill Vladimirovich of Russia">Grand Duke Kirill Vladimirovich</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>There are documents which support the fact that, in this critical situation, the empress dowager Maria Feodorovna was involved in a planned coup d'état to depose her son from the throne in order to save the monarchy.<sup id="cite_ref-King,_Greg_1994._p._299-300_125-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-King,_Greg_1994._p._299-300-125">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> The plan was reportedly for Maria to make a final ultimatum to the Tsar to banish Rasputin unless he wished for her to leave the capital, which would be the signal to unleash the coup.<sup id="cite_ref-King,_Greg_1994._p._299-300_125-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-King,_Greg_1994._p._299-300-125">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> Exactly how she planned to replace her son is unconfirmed, but two versions are available: first, that <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duke_Paul_Alexandrovich_of_Russia" title="Grand Duke Paul Alexandrovich of Russia">Grand Duke Paul Alexandrovich</a> would take power in her name, and that she herself would thereafter become ruling empress; the other version claims that she and Grand Duke Paul Alexandrovich would replace the Tsar with his son, the heir to the throne, Maria's grandson Alexei, upon which Maria and Paul Alexandrovich would share power as regents during his minority.<sup id="cite_ref-King,_Greg_1994._p._299-300_125-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-King,_Greg_1994._p._299-300-125">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> Reportedly, Empress Alexandra was informed about the planned coup, and when Maria Feodorovna made the ultimatum to the Tsar, the empress convinced him to order his mother to leave the capital.<sup id="cite_ref-King,_Greg_1994._p._299-300_125-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-King,_Greg_1994._p._299-300-125">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> Consequently, the Dowager Empress left St. Petersburg to live in the <a href="/wiki/Mariinskyi_Palace" title="Mariinskyi Palace">Mariinskyi Palace</a> in <a href="/wiki/Kiev" class="mw-redirect" title="Kiev">Kiev</a> the same year. She never again returned to Russia's capital.
</p>
<h2><span id="Revolution_.281917.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Revolution_(1917)">Revolution (1917)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alexandra_Feodorovna_(Alix_of_Hesse)&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Revolution (1917)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p><a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a> put what proved to be an unbearable burden on Imperial Russia's government and economy, both of which were dangerously weak. Mass shortages and hunger became the daily situation for tens of millions of Russians due to the disruptions of the <a href="/wiki/War_economy" title="War economy">war economy</a>. Fifteen million men were diverted from agricultural production to fight in the war, and the transportation infrastructure (primarily railroads) was diverted towards war use, exacerbating food shortages in the cities as available agricultural products could not be brought to urban areas. Inflation was rampant. This, combined with the food shortages and the poor performance by the Russian military in the war, generated a great deal of anger and unrest among the people in <a href="/wiki/Saint_Petersburg" title="Saint Petersburg">Saint Petersburg</a> and other cities.<sup id="cite_ref-127" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-127">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The decision of the tsar to take personal command of the military was disastrous, as he was directly blamed for all losses. His relocation to the front, leaving the Empress in charge of the government, helped undermine the Romanov dynasty. The poor performance of the military led to rumours believed by the people that the German-born Empress was part of a conspiracy to help Germany win the war. Moreover, within several months of taking personal command of the army, the tsar replaced several capable ministers with less able men at the Empress's and Rasputin's behest; most notable among these replacements was replacing <a href="/wiki/Nikolai_Borisovich_Shcherbatov" title="Nikolai Borisovich Shcherbatov">N. B. Shcherbatov</a> with <a href="/wiki/Alexei_Khvostov" title="Alexei Khvostov">Alexei Khvostov</a> as minister of the interior.<sup id="cite_ref-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-128">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup> The severe winter of 1916–17 essentially doomed Imperial Russia. Food shortages worsened and <a href="/wiki/Famine" title="Famine">famine</a> gripped the cities. The mismanagement and failures of the war turned the soldiers against the tsar. By 1917, the tsar realized that Russia could not fight the war much longer, and as railroads carried troops to the front there was little capacity left to bring food to the cities.
</p><p>By March 1917, conditions had worsened even more. Steelworkers went out on strike on 7 March, and the following day, crowds hungry for bread began rioting on the streets of Saint Petersburg to protest food shortages and the war. After two days of rioting, the tsar ordered the Army to restore order and on 11 March they fired on the crowd. That very same day, the <a href="/wiki/Duma" title="Duma">Duma</a>, the elected legislature, urged the tsar to take action to ameliorate the concerns of the people. The tsar responded by dissolving the Duma.<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 12 March soldiers sent to suppress the rioting crowds mutinied and joined the rebellion, thus providing the spark to ignite the February Revolution (like the later October Revolution of November 1917, the Russian Revolutions of 1917 get their names due to the Old Style calendar). Soldiers and workers set up the "<a href="/wiki/Petrograd_Soviet" title="Petrograd Soviet">Petrograd Soviet</a>" of 2,500 elected deputies while the Duma declared a Provisional Government on 13 March. <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Kerensky" title="Alexander Kerensky">Alexander Kerensky</a> was a key player in the new regime. The Duma informed the tsar that day that he must abdicate.
</p><p>In an effort to put an end to the uprising in the capital, Nicholas tried to get to Saint Petersburg by train from army headquarters at Mogiliev. The route was blocked so he tried another way. His train was stopped at Pskov where, after receiving advice from his generals, he first <a href="/wiki/Abdication_of_Nicholas_II" title="Abdication of Nicholas II">abdicated the throne</a> for himself and later, on seeking medical advice, for himself and his son the <a href="/wiki/Alexei_Nikolaevich,_Tsarevich_of_Russia" title="Alexei Nikolaevich, Tsarevich of Russia">Tsarevich Alexei</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-130">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Alexandra was now in a perilous position as the wife of the deposed tsar, hated by the Russian people. There were attempts made by the mutinous <a href="/wiki/Tsarskoe_Selo" class="mw-redirect" title="Tsarskoe Selo">Tsarskoe Selo</a> garrison to storm the Alexander Palace at Tsarskoe Selo, but the palace was successfully defended by the palace guards.<sup id="cite_ref-King,_Greg_1994._p_330-335_131-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-King,_Greg_1994._p_330-335-131">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> The Palace guards and other troops gradually left for the capital after being informed about the abdication, and Alexandra asked the Duma to put in place security measures for her and her household in view of the riots and violence in the nearby capital.<sup id="cite_ref-Buxhoeveden,_Sophie_1928_132-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Buxhoeveden,_Sophie_1928-132">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> On 18 March <a href="/wiki/Mikhail_Rodzianko" title="Mikhail Rodzianko">Mikhail Rodzianko</a> sent the newly appointed Minister of War, <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Guchkov" title="Alexander Guchkov">Alexander Guchkov</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Lavr_Kornilov" title="Lavr Kornilov">General Kornilov</a> to Alexandra to inspect the security of the Palace, which resulted in an officer being appointed to maintain the security of the Palace as well as a channel of communication between the Palace and the Duma.<sup id="cite_ref-Buxhoeveden,_Sophie_1928_132-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Buxhoeveden,_Sophie_1928-132">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup>  After this, Alexandra noticed that the guards defending the palace gradually come to wear handkerchiefs around their wrists, signalling that they supported the <a href="/wiki/Duma" title="Duma">Duma</a>, which also meant that she and her children, while being defended from immediate harm, were nevertheless under <a href="/wiki/De_facto" title="De facto">de facto</a> house arrest from that moment on.<sup id="cite_ref-King,_Greg_1994._p_330-335_131-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-King,_Greg_1994._p_330-335-131">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> Alexandra and her children and household were not molested in any way, and the household was left to continue its everyday life as before, with the exception of occasional power cuts.<sup id="cite_ref-King,_Greg_1994_133-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-King,_Greg_1994-133">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup>  On 21 March, Kornilov informed Alexandra that she was formally under house arrest, and the members of the household were informed that they were free to leave if they wished, but if they chose to stay, they would have to obey the same rules as pertained to the house arrest of Alexandra.<sup id="cite_ref-Buxhoeveden,_Sophie_1928_132-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Buxhoeveden,_Sophie_1928-132">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The following day, on 22 March, Nicholas finally was allowed to return to the Alexander Palace at Tsarskoe Selo where he was placed under arrest with his family. Alexandra told him that "the husband and father was of more value in her eyes than the Emperor whose throne she had shared."<sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability#Self-published_sources" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability"><span title="The material near this tag may rely on a self-published source. (November 2021)">self-published source</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Imprisonment_.281917.E2.80.931918.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Imprisonment_(1917–1918)">Imprisonment (1917–1918)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alexandra_Feodorovna_(Alix_of_Hesse)&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Imprisonment (1917–1918)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Empress_Alexandra_with_Olga_and_Tatiana,_last_photo.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/54/Empress_Alexandra_with_Olga_and_Tatiana%2C_last_photo.jpg/180px-Empress_Alexandra_with_Olga_and_Tatiana%2C_last_photo.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="212" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/54/Empress_Alexandra_with_Olga_and_Tatiana%2C_last_photo.jpg/270px-Empress_Alexandra_with_Olga_and_Tatiana%2C_last_photo.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/54/Empress_Alexandra_with_Olga_and_Tatiana%2C_last_photo.jpg/360px-Empress_Alexandra_with_Olga_and_Tatiana%2C_last_photo.jpg 2x" data-file-width="710" data-file-height="837" /></a><figcaption>The last photograph ever taken of Alexandra. With her are her daughters Olga (right) and Tatiana (left). They are sitting on the balcony of the Governor's Mansion, Tobolsk, in Siberia in spring of 1918.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Russian_Provisional_Government" title="Russian Provisional Government">Provisional Government</a> formed after the revolution kept Nicholas, Alexandra, and their children confined under <a href="/wiki/House_arrest" title="House arrest">house arrest</a> in their home, the <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Palace" title="Alexander Palace">Alexander Palace</a> at <a href="/wiki/Tsarskoye_Selo" title="Tsarskoye Selo">Tsarskoye Selo</a>. They were visited by <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Kerensky" title="Alexander Kerensky">Kerensky</a> from the government, who interviewed Alexandra regarding her involvement in state affairs and Rasputin's involvement in them through his influence over her.<sup id="cite_ref-King,_Greg_1994._p_344-345_135-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-King,_Greg_1994._p_344-345-135">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup> She answered that as she and her spouse kept no secrets from each other, they often discussed politics and she naturally gave him advice to support him; as for Rasputin, he had been a true holy man of God, and his advice had been only in the interest of the good of Russia and the imperial family.<sup id="cite_ref-King,_Greg_1994._p_344-345_135-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-King,_Greg_1994._p_344-345-135">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup> After the interview, Kerensky told the tsar that he believed that Alexandra had told him the truth and was not lying.<sup id="cite_ref-King,_Greg_1994_133-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-King,_Greg_1994-133">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Provisional Government did not wish to keep the family in Russia, particularly as both the family as well as the Provincial Government were under threat from the Bolsheviks; they trusted that the former tsar and his family would be received in Great Britain, and ensured that inquiries were being made.<sup id="cite_ref-King,_Greg_1994_133-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-King,_Greg_1994-133">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> Despite the fact he was a first cousin of both Nicholas and Alexandra, <a href="/wiki/George_V" title="George V">George V</a> refused to allow them and their family permission to evacuate to the <a href="/wiki/United_Kingdom_of_Great_Britain_and_Ireland" title="United Kingdom of Great Britain and Ireland">United Kingdom</a>, as he was alarmed by their unpopularity in his country and the potential repercussions to his own throne.<sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup> After this, it was suggested they be moved to France. However, although the French government was never asked, British diplomats in France reported that the family was not likely to be welcome there, as anti-German feelings were strong in France during the war and Alexandra was widely unpopular because she was believed to be a sympathizer of Germany.<sup id="cite_ref-King,_Greg_1994_133-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-King,_Greg_1994-133">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> The Provisional Government was reportedly very disappointed that no foreign state seemed to be willing to receive the family, and was forced to act and relocate them within Russia, as the security situation was becoming more and more difficult.<sup id="cite_ref-King,_Greg_1994_133-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-King,_Greg_1994-133">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In August 1917, the family were moved to <a href="/wiki/Tobolsk" title="Tobolsk">Tobolsk</a> in <a href="/wiki/Siberia" title="Siberia">Siberia</a>, a step by the <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Kerensky" title="Alexander Kerensky">Kerensky</a> government designed to remove them from the capital and possible harm.<sup id="cite_ref-King,_Greg_1994_133-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-King,_Greg_1994-133">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup>  Nicholas and Alexandra had themselves suggested to be moved to the <a href="/wiki/Livadia_Palace" title="Livadia Palace">Livadia Palace</a> in the Crimea, but Kerensky deemed this to be too dangerous: to get to the Crimea, they would have to travel through Central Russia, an area which was at that time affected by widespread revolutionary violence and riots where the upper classes and aristocracy was attacked by the public and their mansions burned.<sup id="cite_ref-King,_Greg_1994_133-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-King,_Greg_1994-133">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> Tobolsk in Siberia was, in contrast to Central and Southern Russia, a calm and peaceful place with greater security and more sympathy for the former tsar.<sup id="cite_ref-King,_Greg_1994_133-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-King,_Greg_1994-133">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> There were indications that the Provisional Government were actually attempting to transport them out of Russia by the <a href="/wiki/Trans-Siberian_Railway" title="Trans-Siberian Railway">Trans-Siberian Railway</a>, thus fulfilling the government's wish to have them expelled, but now via a different route, after the first attempt to exile them to Europe had failed.<sup id="cite_ref-King,_Greg_1994_133-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-King,_Greg_1994-133">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> However, this plan was not revealed to the family, and if it had indeed been the intent of the government, it had to be cancelled because of a strong Bolshevik presence in Ekaterinburg and other cities along the Trans-Siberian Railway east of Tobolsk, and the family therefore continued to their official destination.<sup id="cite_ref-King,_Greg_1994_133-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-King,_Greg_1994-133">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>From Tobolsk, Alexandra managed to send a letter to her sister-in-law, <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duchess_Xenia_Alexandrovna_of_Russia" title="Grand Duchess Xenia Alexandrovna of Russia">Xenia Alexandrovna</a>, in <a href="/wiki/Crimean_Peninsula" class="mw-redirect" title="Crimean Peninsula">Crimea</a>:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>My darling Xenia,
</p><p>My thoughts are with you, how magically good and beautiful everything must be with you – you are the flowers. But it is indescribably painful for the kind motherland, I cannot explain. I am glad for you that you are finally with all your family as you have been apart. I would like to see Olga in all her new big happiness. Everybody is healthy, but myself, during the last 6 weeks I experience nerve pains in my face with toothache. Very tormenting ...
</p><p>
We live quietly, have established ourselves well [in Tobolsk] although it is far, far away from everybody, But God is merciful. He gives us strength and consolation ...<sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Alexandra and her family remained in Tobolsk until after the <a href="/wiki/October_Revolution" title="October Revolution">Bolshevik Revolution</a> in November 1917. The fall of the Provisional Government and the Bolshevik's accession to power greatly worsened their position.<sup id="cite_ref-King,_Greg_1994_133-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-King,_Greg_1994-133">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1918, they were subsequently moved to Bolshevik controlled <a href="/wiki/Yekaterinburg" title="Yekaterinburg">Yekaterinburg</a>. Nicholas, Alexandra and their daughter Maria arrived at the <a href="/wiki/Ipatiev_House" title="Ipatiev House">Ipatiev House</a> on 30 April 1918. On entering their new prison, they were ordered to open all their luggage. Alexandra immediately objected. Nicholas tried to come to her defence saying, "So far we have had polite treatment and men who were gentlemen but now -"<sup id="cite_ref-King,_G_p.344_138-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-King,_G_p.344-138">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> The former Tsar was quickly cut off. The guards informed him he was no longer at Tsarskoe Selo and that refusal to comply with their request would result in his removal from the rest of his family; a second offence would be rewarded with hard labour. Fearing for her husband's safety, Alexandra quickly gave in and allowed the search. On the window frame of what was to be her last bedroom in the Ipatiev House, Alexandra scrawled a <a href="/wiki/Swastika" title="Swastika">swastika</a>, her favourite good luck symbol, and pencilled the date 17/30 April 1918.<sup id="cite_ref-King,_G_p.344_138-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-King,_G_p.344-138">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> In May, the rest of the family arrived in Yekaterinburg. They had not been able to travel earlier due to the illness of Alexei. Alexandra was pleased to be reunited with her family once more.
</p><p>Seventy-five men did guard duty at the Ipatiev House. Many of the men were factory workers from the local Zlokazovsky Factory and the Verkh-Isetsk Factory. The commandant of the Ipatiev House, Alexander Avadeyev was described as "a real Bolshevik". The majority of witnesses recall him as coarse, brutish and a heavy drinker. If a request for a favour on behalf of the family reached Avadeyev, he always gave the same response, "Let them go to hell!" The guards in the house often heard him refer to the deposed tsar as "Nicholas the Blood-Drinker" and to Alexandra as "The German Bitch".<sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>For the Romanovs, life at the Ipatiev House was a nightmare of uncertainty and fear. The imperial family never knew if they would still be in the Ipatiev House from one day to the next or if they might be separated or killed. The privileges allowed to them were few. For an hour each afternoon they could exercise in the rear garden under the watchful eye of the guards. Alexei could still not walk, and his sailor Nagorny had to carry him. Alexandra rarely joined her family in these daily activities. Instead she spent most of her time sitting in a wheelchair, reading the Bible or the works of St. Seraphim. At night the Romanovs played cards or read; they received little mail from the outside world, and the only newspapers they were allowed were outdated editions.<sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Dmitri Volkogonov and other Soviet historians believe that indirect evidence indicates that <a href="/wiki/Vladimir_Lenin" title="Vladimir Lenin">Vladimir Lenin</a> personally ordered the execution of the Imperial Family,<sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> although official Soviet accounts place the responsibility for the decision with the Ural Regional Soviet.<sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Leon_Trotsky" title="Leon Trotsky">Leon Trotsky</a>, in his diary, makes it quite clear that the execution took place on the authority of Lenin. Trotsky wrote:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>My next visit to Moscow took place after the fall of Ekaterinburg. Talking to <a href="/wiki/Yakov_Sverdlov" title="Yakov Sverdlov">Sverdlov</a> I asked in passing, "Oh yes, and where is the tsar?" "It's all over," he answered. "He has been shot." "And where is his family?" "And the family with him." "All of them?" I asked, apparently with a touch of surprise. "All of them," replied Sverdlov. "What about it?" He was waiting to see my reaction. I made no reply. "And who made the decision?" I asked. "We decided it here. Ilyich (Lenin) believed that we shouldn't leave The Whites a live banner to rally around, especially under the present difficult circumstances."<sup id="cite_ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>On 4 July 1918, <a href="/wiki/Yakov_Yurovsky" title="Yakov Yurovsky">Yakov Yurovsky</a>, the chief of the Ekaterinburg Cheka, was appointed commandant of the Ipatiev House. Yurovsky was a loyal Bolshevik, a man Moscow could rely on to carry out its orders regarding the imperial family. Yurovsky quickly tightened security. From the imperial family he collected all of their jewellery and valuables. These he placed in a box which he sealed and left with the prisoners. Alexandra kept only two bracelets which her uncle <a href="/wiki/Prince_Leopold,_Duke_of_Albany" title="Prince Leopold, Duke of Albany">Prince Leopold, Duke of Albany</a>, had given her as a child and which she could not take off. He did not know that the former tsarina and her daughters wore concealed on their person diamonds, emeralds, rubies and ropes of pearls. These would be discovered only after the executions. Yurovsky had been given the order for the execution on 13 July.<sup id="cite_ref-144" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-144">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On Sunday, 14 July 1918, two priests came to the Ipatiev House to celebrate the Divine Liturgy. One of the priests, Father Storozhev later recalled:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>I went into the living room first, then the deacon and Yurovsky. At the same time Nicholas and Alexandra entered through the doors leading into the inner room. Two of his daughters were with him. I did not have a chance to see exactly which ones. I believe Yurovsky asked Nicholas Alexandrovich, "Well, are you all here?" Nicholas Alexandrovich answered firmly, "Yes, all of us." Ahead beyond the archway, Alexandra Feodorovna was already in place with two daughters and Alexei Nicolaievich. He was sitting in a wheelchair and wore a jacket, as it seemed to me, with a sailor's collar. He was pale, but not so much as at the time of my first service. In general he looked more healthy. Alexandra Feodorovna also had a healthier appearance. ...According to the liturgy of the service it is customary at a certain point to read the prayer, "Who Resteth with the Saints." On this occasion for some reason the deacon, instead of reading the prayer began to sing it, and I as well, somewhat embarrassed by this departure from the ritual. But we had scarcely begun to sing when I heard the members of the Romanov family, standing behind me, fall on their knees ...<sup id="cite_ref-145" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-145">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Execution">Execution</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alexandra_Feodorovna_(Alix_of_Hesse)&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Execution">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Murder_of_the_Romanov_family" title="Murder of the Romanov family">Murder of the Romanov family</a></div>
<p>Tuesday, 16 July 1918 passed normally for the former imperial family. At four o'clock in the afternoon, Nicholas and his daughters took their usual walk in the small garden. Early in the evening Yurovsky sent away the fifteen-year-old kitchen boy Leonid Sedinev, saying that his uncle wished to see him. At 7 p.m., Yurovsky summoned all the Cheka men into his room and ordered them to collect all the revolvers from the outside guards. With twelve heavy military revolvers lying before him on the table he said, "Tonight, we shoot the entire family, everybody." Upstairs Nicholas and Alexandra passed the evening playing <a href="/wiki/Bezique" title="Bezique">bezique</a>; at ten thirty, they went to bed.<sup id="cite_ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The former tsar, tsarina, and all of their family, including the gravely ill Alexei, along with several family servants, were executed by firing squad and bayonets in the basement of the Ipatiev House, where they had been imprisoned, early in the morning of 17 July 1918, by a detachment of <a href="/wiki/Bolshevik" class="mw-redirect" title="Bolshevik">Bolsheviks</a> led by <a href="/wiki/Yakov_Yurovsky" title="Yakov Yurovsky">Yakov Yurovsky</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-147" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-147">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup> In the basement room of the Ipatiev House, Alexandra complained that there were no chairs for them to sit on, whereupon Nicholas asked for and received three chairs from the guards. Minutes later, at about 2:15&#160;a.m., a squad of soldiers, each armed with a revolver, entered the room. Their leader Yurovsky ordered the entire party to stand; Alexandra complied "with a flash of anger", and Yurovsky then casually pronounced, "Your relations have tried to save you. They have failed and we must now shoot you." Nicholas rose from his chair and only had time to utter "What...?" before he was shot several times, not (as is usually said) in the head, but in the chest; his skull bears no bullet wounds, but his ribs were shattered by at least three fatal bullet wounds.<sup id="cite_ref-148" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-148">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> Standing about six feet from the gunmen and facing them, Alexandra watched the execution of her husband and two manservants before military commissar <a href="/wiki/Peter_Ermakov" title="Peter Ermakov">Peter Ermakov</a> took aim at her.  She instinctively turned away from him and began to make the sign of the cross, but before she could finish the gesture, Ermakov killed her with a single gunshot which, as she had partly turned away, entered her head just above the left ear and exited at the same spot above her right ear. After all the victims had been shot, Ermakov in a drunken haze stabbed Alexandra's body and that of her husband, shattering both their rib cages and chipping some of Alexandra's vertebrae.<sup id="cite_ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Identification_of_remains">Identification of remains</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alexandra_Feodorovna_(Alix_of_Hesse)&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Identification of remains">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Yekaterinburg_cathedral_on_the_blood_2007.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/Yekaterinburg_cathedral_on_the_blood_2007.jpg/200px-Yekaterinburg_cathedral_on_the_blood_2007.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="300" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/Yekaterinburg_cathedral_on_the_blood_2007.jpg/300px-Yekaterinburg_cathedral_on_the_blood_2007.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/Yekaterinburg_cathedral_on_the_blood_2007.jpg/400px-Yekaterinburg_cathedral_on_the_blood_2007.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2234" data-file-height="3351" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Yekaterinburg" title="Yekaterinburg">Yekaterinburg</a>'s "<a href="/wiki/All_Saints_Resplendent_in_the_Russian_Land" class="mw-redirect" title="All Saints Resplendent in the Russian Land">Church on the Blood</a>", built on the spot where the Ipatiev House once stood</figcaption></figure>
<p>After the execution of the Romanov family in the Ipatiev House, Alexandra's body, along with those of Nicholas, their children and some faithful retainers who died with them, was stripped and the clothing burnt according to the Yurovsky Note, a secret report by Yurovsky, which came to light in the late 1970s, but did not become public knowledge until the 1990s. Initially, the bodies were thrown down a disused mine-shaft at <a href="/wiki/Ganina_Yama" title="Ganina Yama">Ganina Yama</a>, <span style="white-space:nowrap">12&#160;miles&#160;(19&#160;km)</span> north of Yekaterinburg. A short time later, the bodies were retrieved. Their faces were smashed and the bodies, dismembered and disfigured with <a href="/wiki/Sulphuric_acid" class="mw-redirect" title="Sulphuric acid">sulphuric acid</a>, were hurriedly buried under railway sleepers with the exception of two of the children whose bodies were not discovered until 2007. The Yurovsky Note helped the authorities to locate the bodies. The missing bodies were those of a daughter—Maria or Anastasia—and Alexei.<sup id="cite_ref-150" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-150">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> In the early 1990s, following the fall of the <a href="/wiki/Soviet_Union" title="Soviet Union">Soviet Union</a>, the bodies of the majority of the Romanovs were located along with their loyal servants, exhumed and formally identified. Preliminary results of genetic analysis carried out on the remains of a boy and a young woman believed to belong to Nicholas II's son and heir Alexei, and daughter Anastasia or Maria were revealed on 22 January 2008.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_151-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-151">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-152" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-152">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> The Ekaterinburg region's chief forensic expert said, "Tests conducted in Yekaterinburg and Moscow allowed DNA to be extracted from the bones, which proved positive," Nikolai Nevolin said. "Once the genetic analysis has been completed in Russia, its results will be compared with test results from foreign experts."<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_151-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-151">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup> Nevolin said the final results would be published in April or May 2008.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_151-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-151">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup> Certainty about the remains definitively put an end to the claim that <a href="/wiki/Anna_Anderson" title="Anna Anderson">Anna Anderson</a> could be connected with the Romanovs, as all remaining bodies would be accounted for.
</p><p>DNA analysis represented a key means of identifying the bodies. A blood sample from <a href="/wiki/Prince_Philip,_Duke_of_Edinburgh" title="Prince Philip, Duke of Edinburgh">Prince Philip, Duke of Edinburgh</a> (a grandson of Alexandra's oldest sister, <a href="/wiki/Princess_Victoria_of_Hesse_and_by_Rhine" title="Princess Victoria of Hesse and by Rhine">Princess Victoria of Hesse and by Rhine</a>) was employed to identify Alexandra and her daughters through their <a href="/wiki/Mitochondrial_DNA" title="Mitochondrial DNA">mitochondrial DNA</a>. They belonged to <a href="/wiki/Haplogroup_H_(mtDNA)" title="Haplogroup H (mtDNA)">Haplogroup H (mtDNA)</a>. Nicholas was identified using DNA obtained from, among others, his late brother <a href="/wiki/Grand_Duke_George_Alexandrovich_of_Russia" title="Grand Duke George Alexandrovich of Russia">Grand Duke George Alexandrovich of Russia</a>. Grand Duke George had died of <a href="/wiki/Tuberculosis" title="Tuberculosis">tuberculosis</a> in the late 1890s and was buried in the Peter and Paul Fortress in St Petersburg.<sup id="cite_ref-153" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-153">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-154" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-154">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-155" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-155">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Burial">Burial</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alexandra_Feodorovna_(Alix_of_Hesse)&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Burial">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Alexandra, <a href="/wiki/Nicholas_II_of_Russia" title="Nicholas II of Russia">Nicholas II</a> and three daughters plus the servants who were killed with them were reinterred in the St. Catherine Chapel of the <a href="/wiki/Saints_Peter_and_Paul_Cathedral,_Saint_Petersburg" title="Saints Peter and Paul Cathedral, Saint Petersburg">Peter and Paul Cathedral</a> at the Fortress of St. Peter and St. Paul in <a href="/wiki/Saint_Petersburg" title="Saint Petersburg">St. Petersburg</a> in 1998, with much ceremony, on the eightieth anniversary of the execution.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Sainthood">Sainthood</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alexandra_Feodorovna_(Alix_of_Hesse)&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Sainthood">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"><table class="infobox vcard"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-above n" style="background-color:gold;"><div style="display:inline;" class="fn">Saint Alexandra Romanova of Russia</div></th></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background-color:gold;"><a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">Saint</a>, <a href="/wiki/Passion-Bearer" class="mw-redirect" title="Passion-Bearer">Passion-Bearer</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tsaritsa" class="mw-redirect" title="Tsaritsa">Tsaritsa</a></th></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Venerated&#160;in</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodoxy" title="Eastern Orthodoxy">Eastern Orthodoxy</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Canonization" title="Canonization">Canonized</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1126788409"><div class="plainlist"><ul><li>1981 by <a href="/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church_Abroad" class="mw-redirect" title="Russian Orthodox Church Abroad">Russian Orthodox Church Abroad</a></li><li>2000 by the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church" title="Russian Orthodox Church">Russian Orthodox Church</a></li></ul></div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><span class="nowrap">Major <a href="/wiki/Shrine" title="Shrine">shrine</a></span></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Church_of_All_Saints,_Yekaterinburg" title="Church of All Saints, Yekaterinburg">Church on Blood</a>, Yekaterinburg, Russia</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints" title="Calendar of saints">Feast</a></th><td class="infobox-data">17 July</td></tr></tbody></table>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Canonization_of_the_Romanovs" title="Canonization of the Romanovs">Canonization of the Romanovs</a></div>
<p>In 1981, Alexandra and her immediate family were recognised as <a href="/wiki/Martyr" title="Martyr">martyrs</a> by the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church_Outside_Russia" class="mw-redirect" title="Russian Orthodox Church Outside Russia">Russian Orthodox Church Outside Russia</a>. In 2000, Alexandra was <a href="/wiki/Canonization" title="Canonization">canonized</a> as a <a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">saint</a> and <a href="/wiki/Passion_bearer" title="Passion bearer">passion bearer</a> by the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church" title="Russian Orthodox Church">Russian Orthodox Church</a>, together with her husband Nicholas II, their children and others including her sister <a href="/wiki/Princess_Elisabeth_of_Hesse_and_by_Rhine_(1864%E2%80%931918)" title="Princess Elisabeth of Hesse and by Rhine (1864–1918)">Grand Duchess Elisabeth Feodorovna</a> and the Grand Duchess's fellow nun <a href="/wiki/Barbara_(Yakovleva)" title="Barbara (Yakovleva)">Varvara</a>.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="In_popular_culture">In popular culture</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alexandra_Feodorovna_(Alix_of_Hesse)&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: In popular culture">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<ul><li>The best-selling 1895 American novel <i><a href="/wiki/The_Princess_Aline" title="The Princess Aline">The Princess Aline</a></i> by <a href="/wiki/Richard_Harding_Davis" title="Richard Harding Davis">Richard Harding Davis</a> was based on his infatuation with Alexandra.<sup id="cite_ref-hisday_156-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-hisday-156">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><i><a href="/wiki/Rasputin_and_the_Empress" title="Rasputin and the Empress">Rasputin and the Empress</a></i> (1932), a fictionalized film less famous than the lawsuit it spawned. Alexandra was portrayed by <a href="/wiki/Ethel_Barrymore" title="Ethel Barrymore">Ethel Barrymore</a>.</li>
<li>The highly fictionalized 1966 film <i><a href="/wiki/Rasputin,_the_Mad_Monk" class="mw-redirect" title="Rasputin, the Mad Monk">Rasputin, the Mad Monk</a></i>, in which <a href="/wiki/Ren%C3%A9e_Asherson" title="Renée Asherson">Renée Asherson</a> portrayed the Empress.</li>
<li>A rather romanticised version of Alexandra's life was dramatized in the 1971 movie <i><a href="/wiki/Nicholas_and_Alexandra" title="Nicholas and Alexandra">Nicholas and Alexandra</a></i>, based on the book by the same title written by <a href="/wiki/Robert_Massie" class="mw-redirect" title="Robert Massie">Robert Massie</a>, in which the tsaritsa/Empress was played by <a href="/wiki/Janet_Suzman" title="Janet Suzman">Janet Suzman</a>.</li>
<li>The song "<a href="/wiki/Rasputin_(song)" title="Rasputin (song)">Rasputin</a>" is a 1978 Euro-disco hit single by the Germany-based group <a href="/wiki/Boney_M" class="mw-redirect" title="Boney M">Boney M</a>. It tells of Alexandra's alleged affair with Rasputin.</li>
<li>1974's <i><a href="/wiki/Fall_of_Eagles" title="Fall of Eagles">Fall of Eagles</a></i>, a BBC series dramatizing the demise of the <a href="/wiki/Dynasty" title="Dynasty">ruling families</a> of <a href="/wiki/German_Empire" title="German Empire">Germany</a>, <a href="/wiki/Austria-Hungary" title="Austria-Hungary">Austria-Hungary</a> and <a href="/wiki/Russian_Empire" title="Russian Empire">Russia</a>. Alexandra, portrayed by American actress <a href="/wiki/Gayle_Hunnicutt" title="Gayle Hunnicutt">Gayle Hunnicutt</a>, is a prominent character in the series.</li>
<li><i><a href="/wiki/Rasputin:_Dark_Servant_of_Destiny" title="Rasputin: Dark Servant of Destiny">Rasputin: Dark Servant of Destiny</a></i> is a 1996 <a href="/wiki/HBO" title="HBO">HBO</a> <a href="/wiki/TV_film" class="mw-redirect" title="TV film">TV film</a> for which <a href="/wiki/Greta_Scacchi" title="Greta Scacchi">Greta Scacchi</a> won an <a href="/wiki/Emmy_Award" class="mw-redirect" title="Emmy Award">Emmy</a> for her portrayal of Empress Alexandra.</li>
<li><i>Rasputin: The Mad Monk</i> (1997), a biographical documentary.</li>
<li><i><a href="/wiki/Anastasia_(1997_film)" title="Anastasia (1997 film)">Anastasia</a></i> (1997), an American animated musical fantasy drama film produced by Fox Animation Studios, where Empress Alexandra appears in flashbacks.</li>
<li><i><a href="/wiki/The_Romanovs:_An_Imperial_Family" title="The Romanovs: An Imperial Family">The Romanovs: An Imperial Family</a></i> (2000), a Russian film that explores the last year of the Imperial family after Nicholas's abdication, the house arrest of the family and eventual execution. <a href="/wiki/Lynda_Bellingham" title="Lynda Bellingham">Lynda Bellingham</a> plays Empress Alexandra.</li>
<li><i><a href="/wiki/The_Lost_Prince" title="The Lost Prince">The Lost Prince</a></i>, a BBC mini-series made in 2003 about <a href="/wiki/Prince_John_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Prince John of the United Kingdom">Prince John of the United Kingdom</a>, the youngest son of King George V, in which Alexandra is played by Lithuanian actress <a href="/wiki/Ingeborga_Dapk%C5%ABnait%C4%97" title="Ingeborga Dapkūnaitė">Ingeborga Dapkūnaitė</a>.</li>
<li>The episode "Love and Revolution" devoted to the fall of the Romanov dynasty is featured in the Danish television <i>A Royal Family</i>, a series about the descendants of King Christian IX of Denmark.</li>
<li>Alexandra Feodorovna is a main character in the stage play <i>Ekaterinburg</i> by David Logan.<sup id="cite_ref-157" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-157">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The 2019 <a href="/wiki/Netflix" title="Netflix">Netflix</a> <a href="/wiki/Docudrama" title="Docudrama">docudrama</a> <i><a href="/wiki/The_Last_Czars" title="The Last Czars">The Last Czars</a></i> explores the reign, and eventual demise, of Tsar Nicholas II. Alexandra was portrayed by Susanna Herbert.</li>
<li>The execution of the Romanov family is depicted in Season 5, Episode 6 of <a href="/wiki/Netflix" title="Netflix">Netflix</a> <a href="/wiki/Docudrama" title="Docudrama">docudrama</a> <i><a href="/wiki/The_Crown_(TV_series)" title="The Crown (TV series)">The Crown</a></i>, which is titled "Ipatiev House."</li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Honors">Honors</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alexandra_Feodorovna_(Alix_of_Hesse)&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Honors">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="National_decorations">National decorations</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alexandra_Feodorovna_(Alix_of_Hesse)&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: National decorations">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>

<li><span class="flagicon"><span class="mw-image-border" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Flag_of_Russia_%281858%E2%80%931883%29.svg/23px-Flag_of_Russia_%281858%E2%80%931883%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="23" height="15" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Flag_of_Russia_%281858%E2%80%931883%29.svg/35px-Flag_of_Russia_%281858%E2%80%931883%29.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/Flag_of_Russia_%281858%E2%80%931883%29.svg/45px-Flag_of_Russia_%281858%E2%80%931883%29.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="900" data-file-height="600" /></span></span>&#160;</span><a href="/wiki/Russian_Empire" title="Russian Empire">Russian Empire</a>: Dame Grand Cross of the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_St._Catherine" class="mw-redirect" title="Order of St. Catherine">Order of St. Catherine</a>, <i>April 1894</i><sup id="cite_ref-handbuch_159-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-handbuch-159">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_decorations">Foreign decorations</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alexandra_Feodorovna_(Alix_of_Hesse)&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Foreign decorations">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>

<li><span class="flagicon"><span class="mw-image-border" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/Flag_of_Prussia_%281892-1918%29.svg/23px-Flag_of_Prussia_%281892-1918%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="23" height="14" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/Flag_of_Prussia_%281892-1918%29.svg/35px-Flag_of_Prussia_%281892-1918%29.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5b/Flag_of_Prussia_%281892-1918%29.svg/46px-Flag_of_Prussia_%281892-1918%29.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="900" data-file-height="540" /></span></span>&#160;</span><a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Prussia" title="Kingdom of Prussia">Kingdom of Prussia</a>: Dame of the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Louise" title="Order of Louise">Order of Louise</a>, 1st Division, <i>5 September 1896</i><sup id="cite_ref-prus2_163-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-prus2-163">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><span class="flagicon"><span class="mw-image-border" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8e/Flag_of_the_Ottoman_Empire_%281844%E2%80%931922%29.svg/23px-Flag_of_the_Ottoman_Empire_%281844%E2%80%931922%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="23" height="15" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8e/Flag_of_the_Ottoman_Empire_%281844%E2%80%931922%29.svg/35px-Flag_of_the_Ottoman_Empire_%281844%E2%80%931922%29.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8e/Flag_of_the_Ottoman_Empire_%281844%E2%80%931922%29.svg/45px-Flag_of_the_Ottoman_Empire_%281844%E2%80%931922%29.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1200" data-file-height="800" /></span></span>&#160;</span><a href="/wiki/Ottoman_Empire" title="Ottoman Empire">Ottoman Empire</a>: Dame Grand Cordon of the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Charity_(Ottoman_Empire)" class="mw-redirect" title="Order of Charity (Ottoman Empire)">Order of Charity</a>, <i>1 July 1902</i><sup id="cite_ref-164" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-164">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><span class="datasortkey" data-sort-value="Spain"><span class="flagicon"><span class="mw-image-border" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7d/Flag_of_Spain_%281785%E2%80%931873%2C_1875%E2%80%931931%29.svg/23px-Flag_of_Spain_%281785%E2%80%931873%2C_1875%E2%80%931931%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="23" height="15" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7d/Flag_of_Spain_%281785%E2%80%931873%2C_1875%E2%80%931931%29.svg/35px-Flag_of_Spain_%281785%E2%80%931873%2C_1875%E2%80%931931%29.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7d/Flag_of_Spain_%281785%E2%80%931873%2C_1875%E2%80%931931%29.svg/45px-Flag_of_Spain_%281785%E2%80%931873%2C_1875%E2%80%931931%29.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1200" data-file-height="800" /></span></span>&#160;</span><a href="/wiki/Restoration_(Spain)" title="Restoration (Spain)">Spain</a></span>: Dame of the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Queen_Maria_Luisa" title="Order of Queen Maria Luisa">Order of Queen Maria Luisa</a>, <i>23 April 1896</i><sup id="cite_ref-165" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-165">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><span class="datasortkey" data-sort-value="United Kingdom"><span class="flagicon"><span class="mw-image-border" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/a/ae/Flag_of_the_United_Kingdom.svg/23px-Flag_of_the_United_Kingdom.svg.png" decoding="async" width="23" height="12" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/a/ae/Flag_of_the_United_Kingdom.svg/35px-Flag_of_the_United_Kingdom.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/a/ae/Flag_of_the_United_Kingdom.svg/46px-Flag_of_the_United_Kingdom.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1200" data-file-height="600" /></span></span>&#160;</span><a href="/wiki/United_Kingdom_of_Great_Britain_and_Ireland" title="United Kingdom of Great Britain and Ireland">United Kingdom</a></span>:
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Queen_Victoria_Golden_Jubilee_Medal" title="Queen Victoria Golden Jubilee Medal">Queen Victoria Golden Jubilee Medal</a>, <i>1887</i><sup id="cite_ref-handbuch_159-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-handbuch-159">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup></li>

<li><span class="flagicon"><span class="mw-image-border" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e3/Early_20th_Century_Qajar_Flag.svg/20px-Early_20th_Century_Qajar_Flag.svg.png" decoding="async" width="20" height="15" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e3/Early_20th_Century_Qajar_Flag.svg/31px-Early_20th_Century_Qajar_Flag.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e3/Early_20th_Century_Qajar_Flag.svg/40px-Early_20th_Century_Qajar_Flag.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="450" /></span></span></span> <a href="/wiki/Qajar_Iran" title="Qajar Iran">Persian Empire</a>: <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Aftab" title="Order of Aftab">Order of the Sun</a>, 1st Class, <i>1900</i><sup id="cite_ref-167" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-167">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Archives">Archives</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alexandra_Feodorovna_(Alix_of_Hesse)&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Archives">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Alexandra Feodorovna's letters to Anna Vyrubova and Lili Dehn, written in the years 1916–1918, are preserved in the "Romanov collection" in the Beinecke Rare Book and Manuscript Library, Yale University (New Haven, Connecticut, USA).<sup id="cite_ref-168" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-168">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Documents about Alexandra and her family (including photographs and correspondence) can also be found in the Archive of the House of Hesse, which is kept in Fasanerie Palace in <a href="/wiki/Eichenzell" title="Eichenzell">Eichenzell</a>, Germany.<sup id="cite_ref-169" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-169">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Ancestry">Ancestry</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alexandra_Feodorovna_(Alix_of_Hesse)&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Ancestry">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
